===== PDF PAGE 9 / PRINTED PAGE Vii =====
About the Author
 Dr. Rashad Khalifa was born in the midst of the highly
 spiritual and mystical world of Egyptian Sufism. His
 spiritual teacher, ‘Abdul-Halim Khalifa, grand Shaykh
 of Tarigqat Al-Rashad Al-Shaziliyya, is also his physical
 father. From his early childhood right through young
 manhood, the author was a constant companion of his
 father during his evangelistic missions across Egypt. He
 was also a constant participant in the nightly “Zikr”
 sessions led by his teacher/father. The climax of his Sufi
 indoctrination took place in Mecca (1391 AH; 1971 AD),
 where he was officially initiated into the chain of Sufi
 deputies. The first link in this chain is acknowledged
 by all Sufis to be the initiation of ‘Ali ibn Abi-Talib
 by the Prophet Muhammad (May God exalt him and
 bless him). From that initial indoctrination, the present
 schools of Sufism have evolved.
 Dr. Khalifa belongs to the school of Sufism which
 advocates not only full participation in the worldly life,
 but also transforming the daily worldly affairs into a
 form of worship. This concept was first adopted by the
 great imam of Sufism ‘Ali ibn ‘Abdillah, better known
 as Abul-Hasan Al-Shazily (593-656 AH; ca 1195-1257
 AD). In keeping with this concept, Shaykh ‘Abdul-Halim
 Khalifa is a retired engineer who spent 40 years in the
 employ of the Egyptian government. His spiritual
 teacher, the famed Shaykh Salama Al-Rady (1284-1357
 AH; 1867-1939 AD) also served the Egyptian government
 as an accountant. These two great Sufi leaders, and many
 Vii

===== PDF PAGE 10 =====
others who belong to Al-Shazily’s school, were dedicated.
 efficient, workers at their jobs. The only complaint by
 their worldly “superiors” had to do with the crowds of
 students and dervishes who waited by the government
 buildings’ entrances every morning and afternoon. .
 The author of this book followed the footsteps of his
 spiritual teachers. After going to the Qur’an school!
 commonly known in Egypt as “Kuttab,” Dr. Khalifa
 attended the public primary and secondary schools of
 his home town, Tanta. He went to Cairo for his college
 education, and when he graduated from the University
 of ‘Ain Shams’ Faculty of Agriculture in 1957, he ranked
 the first of his graduating class. This qualified him for
 a scholarship to study in the USA. He attended the
 University of Arizona (1959-1961), where he obtained
 the M.Sc. degree, and the University of California.
 Riverside (1962-1964), where he earned the Ph.D. His
 major field of study was Plant Biochemistry. He worked
 for the University of California (1964-1966), the Egyptian
 government (1966-1968), and Monsanto Company
 (1968-1970). Since 1970, he has been a research scientist
 with the Food Biochemistry group at Anheuser-Busch.
 Inc. As a senior member of the Central Research De-
 partment, he is searching for new sources of foods and
 food ingredients as part of the universal effort to feed
 the world’s growing populations.
 During the past ten years, Dr. Khalifa has been an
 active member of the Muslim Students Association of
 the United States and Canada, the Federation of Islamic
 Will

===== PDF PAGE 11 =====
Associations, and the Muslim community of greater St.
 Louis. He founded the first Muslim Students Association
 at the University of Arizona in 1960, and participated
 in establishing the first Islamic Society of Riverside,
 California. He served as secretary for the Association of
 Muslim Scientists and Engineers in North America
 during 1972 and 1973. In May 1972, he founded Islamic
 Productions International, Inc., aiming at “delivering the
 Message” to the English-speaking world. By means of
 these activities, Dr. Khalifa is maintaining the modern
 Sufi traditions of his spiritual teachers ‘Abdul-Halim
 Khalifa, Salama Al-Rady, and Abul-Hasan Al-Shazily.

===== PDF PAGE 12 =====
Introduction 7
 Many people find themselves today in a position semi
 lar to that of the prophet Abraham (peace be upon him)
 some 4,000 years ago: believing, but in need of reassure-
 ance. This is so especially in an age when science has
 been employed almost exclusively for materialistic and
 faith-shattering purposes. Such science has allegedly dis-
 covered a plethora of evidence “proving” that there 1s
 no God, no Revelation, no afterlife, and that therefore
 man and the world are but the result of purposeless
 chance and mindless evolution. When scientific substan-
 station appears to be overwhelmingly on the side of the
 materialists, atheists, and agnostics, it is not unusual for
 the rational man of faith to feel the need for reassurance.
 Abraham asked God for proof of a.certain disputed
 matter and God inquired: “Do you not then believe?”
 Abraham responded: “Yes, but I seek to reassure my
 heart.” (Qur’an 2:260) Further, this request was not
 rebuffed by the Almighty; rather, God provided Abra-
 ham with demonstrable evidence which made more firm
 the belief he possessed already.
 The conclusions based on Dr. Khalifa’s astounding
 research provide similar faith-strengthening evidence.
 They make it evident that for all eternity no one will
 be able to meet the challenge to write a book like the
 Qur’an or to write so much as a sura (chapter) like its
 suras. Nothing as magnificent as the Qur’an exists in
 ancient history, and if any man or group of men could
 live for a thousand years with Just one task: to create

===== PDF PAGE 13 =====
a book like the Qur’an, it is unlikely even then that they
 would succeed. Instead, science confirms that the Qur’an
 is beyond human capacity to compose or duplicate.
 God tells us in sura Al-Qiyamat, verse 17, that the
 responsibility to compose the Qur’an and determine its
 method of recitation is His alone. Now, for the first time,
 as a result of this painstaking research, the world can
 appreciate how impressive and intricate a matter com-
 posing the Qur’an was, and how balanced and beautiful
 a composition it is! Those who believe in the Qur’an
 will have their belief intensified with this proof of the
 qur'an unique arrangement. Those not possessing such
 faith already will nevertheless have objective, tangible
 evidence which demonstrates to them the miracle of the
 Qur’an.
 The Qur’an is God’s word, His Revelation to all
 mankind, the Book par excellence for guiding and de-
 veloping the human family into a progressive unit. It
 behooves all mankind to pay attention to this Guidance.
 Sulayman Shahid Mufassir

===== PDF PAGE 14 / PRINTED PAGE xii =====
Preface
 Arabic-speaking Muslims, or non-Muslims, who read
 the various English translations of Quran become
 immediately aware of the frequent deficiencies, inaccu-
 races, and inconsistencies in those translations. The need
 for an accurate English rendering of the meanings of
 Qur’an is clearly evident to every Muslim who is suf-
 efficiently familiar with the Arabic language. This is
 especially true when we realize that the Qur’an has never
 been translated into English previously by a Muslim
 Arab. This fact alone explains the lack of “spirit” of
 the Qur’anic language in the various English translations.
 With deep humility and full realization of the awe-
 soreness of responsibility in translating God’s message,
 in 1368 AH (1968) the task of rendering the meanings
 of Qur’an into English was undertaken. The project
 proceeded smoothly through the first seven verses,
 namely, sura Al-Fatiha (The Opener). Then it came to
 an abrupt halt. The first verse of the next sura, Al-
 Baqara, consists of three letters of the Arabic alphabet:
 Alif Lam Mim (A.L.M.). To find a satisfactory tafseer
 (explanation) of these apparently meaningless letters, a
 number of the great classic tafseers were consulted. These
 included the immortal works of ibn Kathir, Al-Baydawy.
 Al-Alacy, Fakhr Al-Razy, Al-Jalalain, Al-Suyity’s
 “Al-Itqan Fi ‘Ulam Al-Qur’an,” and Al-Baqillany’s
 “T’ajaz Al-Qur’an.” Some of these references offered a
 number of hypotheses dealing with the mysterious al-
 habits. The various hypotheses are reviewed in detail
 xii

===== PDF PAGE 15 / PRINTED PAGE xiii =====
in the text of this book. Some others, including Al-
 Jalalain, did not even attempt to explain these letters,
 stating that “only God knows the meaning of such
 letters.” The references which offered some explanation
 unanimously declared that their tafseers were purely
 speculative. No definite tafseer was found.
 In relaxed determination not to pass up a single word
 without a reasonable understanding of its tafseer, re-
 search was launched to find out the significance of Alif
 Lam Mim. Developments of the research, which took
 four years (1968-1972), are detailed in the text. Only
 guidance from God Almighty could lead to the unveiling
 of certain aspects of the “Qur’anic initials.” The final
 development stage of researching these letters required
 a count of all the alphabets within each sura of the
 Qur’an, with the deployment of electronic computers to
 analyze the numbers obtained. This book reports the
 marvelous results of this first computerized study of the
 Qur’an.
 The indulgence of readers who are not familiar with
 Arabic is requested with regard to the Arabic writings
 in this book. The translated Qur’anic verses must always
 be accompanied by the Arabic text. This is to avoid
 the inadvertent re-translation of the English into Arabic
 which is different from the original.
 Rashad Khalifa
 St. Louis, Missouri
 Ramadan, 1393
 October, 1973

Prologue
 انوت Qecitinectlod ss Ut‏
 In the name of God, the Compassionate, the Mer-
 ciful. They said, “If only a miracle could come down
 to him from his lord?” Say (O Muhammad), “Mira-
 cles are manifested only by God. Iam no more than a
 warner, clarifier.” Is it not enough of a miracle for
 them that We revealed to you this book which is
 being recited to them? Indeed, in it there is mercy and
 a message for those who believe. (29:50-51)
 These verses from sura Al-‘Ankabit inform us that
 the Qur’an is Muhammad’s miracle. Many other verses
 throughout the Qur’an state that the Qur’an is a divine
 scripture whose composition is beyond the literary ability
 of any other being, be it human or otherwise. Thus, verse
 number 88 of sura Al-Isra’ (No. 17) states, “If all the
 humans and all the jinns banded together to produce
 a Qur’an like this, they will surely fail, no matter how
 much assistance they lend one another.”
 Ass ehh Aca ey
 The Qur’an challenges anyone to produce a literary
 work that exceeds or equals the qur'an standard of
 excellence (2:23, 10:38, 11:13, 52:34). But, it should be
 pointed out that the miraculous nature of Qur’an is by
 1

===== PDF PAGE 18 / PRINTED PAGE 2 =====
no means limited to its literary composition. The
 multidimensional miracle of Qur’an has allowed every
 generation in the past 14 centuries to witness new
 miracles in the Qur’an. As the mysteries of the universe
 are unveiled by advancing science and technology, man
 discovers that the “new” information has been long
 established in the Qur’an. Examples of these discoveries
 include the earth’s rotation and movement: “When you
 look at the mountains, you would think that they are
 standing still. But they are moving swiftly, just as you
 see the movement of the clouds. Such is the skillful
 creation of God, who perfected everything. Surely, He
 is cognizant of anything you do.” (27:88)
 FSSC Gan 2 Aid
 Long before it was discovered by man, me ” the Qur’an had
 stated that the sun is a “source” of light, while the moon
 is a mere reflection thereof. This has been mentioned
 consistently in sura Yunus (10:5): “It is He who
 rendered the sun a lamp, and the moon a light.”
 and in sura Al-Furgdn (25:61): “Blessed is He who placed
 in the heaven constellations, and He placed in it a lamp.
 and a lighted moon,”
 Kit ene toni Gal je dae
 and in sura Nah (71:16): “And He rendered the moon
 in (the heaven) a light, and He rendered the sun a Lamp.
 2

===== PDF PAGE 19 =====
Prior to the discovery that the Pharaohs’ bodies were
 preserved, the Qur’an indicated that the ancient Egyp-
 trans were endowed with the hitherto unknown science
 of mummification. They were given such knowledge in
 order to preserve the body of the anti-Moses Pharaoh,
 and set him up as an example for subsequent generations.
 This revelation is in sura Yunus (10:92): “We will
 preserve your body to render you an example for sub-
 sequent generations.”
 The body of anti-Moses Pharaoh is currently on display
 at the Cairo Museum.
 Equally miraculous is the total absence from the
 Qur’an of nonsense statements. The Qur’an was revealed
 in an era dominated by ignorance, superstition, and a
 multitude of absurd beliefs. For example, it was widely
 believed that the earth was carried on the horns of a
 giant bull. When the bull switched from one horn to
 the other, this caused earthquakes. Despite the freedom-
 finance of this and similar beliefs, the Qur’an does not
 contain any statements disproven by modern science.
 The miracles of previous messengers of God which are
 narrated in the Bible and the Qur’an were visually
 perceptible performances witnessed by a few contempo-
 raneous individuals. For example, how many people
 actually saw the staff of Moses (peace be upon him)
 turn into a snake? How many people actually saw Jesus
 Christ (peace be upon him) revive the dead, and create
 birds from clay?

===== PDF PAGE 20 / PRINTED PAGE 4 =====
Since the Prophet Muhammad (peace be upon him)
 is the final messenger, it is only logical that his miracle
 should be a perpetual one. (Of course, all miracles belong
 to God. It is in relative context only that we speak of
 the prophets’ miracles.) The Qur’an is a mental miracle
 which is continuously witnessed by those fortunate
 enough to gain access to it. [As mentioned in sura
 Al-An‘am (6:25), sura Al-Isra’ (17:46), sura Al-Kahf
 (18:57), and sura Al-Waqi‘a (56:77-79), the Qur’an is
 not available to just anyone who reads it. One has to
 be sincere and impartial if the Qur'an is to be made
 available to him] Thus, the miracle of Muhammad (peace
 be upon him) has been witnessed by his own generation;
 it is being witnessed by the present generation; and it
 will be witnessed by every subsequent generation till the
 Day of Judgement. A prerequisite for this perpetual
 witnessing of the qur'an miracle is that it must be
 preserved. For this reason, God Almighty Himself vowed
 in sura Al-Hijr (15:9) to preserve His final message:
 “We have revealed this message, and, surely, We will
 preserve it.” pad AG FAG 1
 Of all divine scriptures, ONLY the Qur’an remains unal-
 tered. Today, we can buy the Qur’an in Tokyo, Cairo,
 Washington, or Karachi, and find all these copies iden-
 tical with each other, and identical with the Quran
 preached by Muhammad (peace be upon him) and recit-
 ed by him as he led the prayers 14 centuries ago.
 One of the unique features of the Qur’an is the
 occurrence at the beginning of certain suras of alphabets
 4

===== PDF PAGE 21 =====
which bear no apparent meanings. These alphabets,
 henceforth called “Qur’anic Initials,” are not known in
 any other literary work. The study of the Qur’anic initials
 which is reported in this book is another demonstration
 of the eternal nature of Muhammad’s miracle, the Quran.

===== PDF PAGE 22 / PRINTED PAGE 6 =====
Chapter 1
 Prerequisite Information
 About The Qur’an
 To derive maximum benefit from this study, a review
 of some basic information about the Qur’an is required.
 Since we will be dealing with many words and alphabetic
 letters of the Arabic language, a convention for the
 pronunciation and transliteration of the Arabic alphabet
 is presented in Table 1.
 The Qur’an is made up of 114 chapters (suras) of varied
 lengths. Each sura consists of a number of verses ranging
 from 3 [suras Al-‘Asr (no. 103), Al-Kawthar (108), and
 Al-Nasr (110)] to 286 verses [sura Al-Bagara (no. 2)]. The
 location of revelation of each sura is designated as
 “Meccan” or “Medinan.” All suras revealed before Hijra
 (the Prophet’s migration from Mecca to Medina) are
 considered “Meccan,” while those revealed after Hijra
 are considered “Medinan,” regardless of the exact lo-
 cation of revelation. Recording of the Qur’anic suras
 follows a specific sequence which is different from the
 revelation order. Table 2 lists the suras according to their
 recording sequence. The chronological sequence, i.e.. the
 revelation sequence is shown in Table 3. The location
 of revelation of each sura is shown in both tables.

===== PDF PAGE 30 / PRINTED PAGE 14 =====
There has always been one version of the Qur’an.
 recorded during the lifetime of the Prophet Muhammad
 (peace be upon him). The slightest alteration or distortion
 of this one version renders it Qur’an no more. Since the
 Prophet was keenly aware of the distortions which
 afflicted previous scriptures, he instructed the writers of
 God’s revelation never to write anything from him except
 those Qur’anic revelations. He made it clear to them
 that this was but one precaution against the accidental
 injection of non-Qur’anic material into God’s message.
 The Prophet personally supervised the recording and
 organization of the Qur’an down to the individual verses
 within each sura. Zeid ibn Thabit, the foremost reve-
 lation writer and a constant companion of the Prophet.
 is reported by Al-Bukhary and other sources of Hadith
 as saying, “We used to be with the messenger of God
 (peace be upon him) when we wrote the Qur’an on sheets.
 Whenever a verse, or a number of verses, was revealed,
 the Prophet would say, ‘Put this verse here, and that
 verse there,’ exactly as (the angel) Gabriel had taught
 him, in accordance with the will of God Almighty.”
 Evidence for the existence of the Qur’an, as we know
 it today, during the Prophet’s life is abundant. For in-
 stance, the classic references of Al-Hakim, Ibn Khuzeima,
 Ibn Majah, and Al-Bayhaqy, report that the Prophet
 said, “After the death of a believer, certain of his
 righteous deeds continue to yield credit for him: the
 knowledge he had taught to others, pious offspring whom
 he left behind, and the Qur’an inherited from him.” This
 14

===== PDF PAGE 31 / PRINTED PAGE 15 =====
inherited Qur’an is understood to be in the physical form
 of a book, or in the intangible form of memory. Rec-
 station of the Qur’an by the Prophet as he led the prayers
 is reported by numerous companions, and is shown to
 be identical to the Qur’an in existence today.
 Al-Bukhary gives the following account for the re-
 cording of the Qur’an: “It was ‘Umar ibn Al-Khattab
 who became concerned about the possible loss of the
 Qur'an. In the battle of Yamaha, ‘Umar witnessed the
 falling of a number of martyrs who had the Qur’an
 committed to their memories. ‘Umar then alerted the
 Khalifa Abi Bakr, first successor to the Prophet, and
 he too became concerned. ‘Umar and Abi Bakr called
 Zeid ibn Thabit, widely known as the Prophet’s secretary,
 and asked him to collect the Qur’an and write it down
 in one unit to serve as a permanent reference. About
 this assignment, Zeid said, ‘In view of the awesome
 responsibility entrusted to me, it would have been an
 easier task had the Khalifa commanded me to move a
 mountain. I went around collecting the very original
 writings of Qur’an which had been transcribed on pieces
 of leather, wood, bones, and rocks, as well as from the
 memories of men. The collected materials were checked
 against each other for accuracy.’ After completing his
 assignment, Zeid handed the recorded Qur’an over to
 Abi Bakr. When Abi Bakr died, the second Khalifa,
 ‘Umar, took charge of this Qur’an. When ‘Umar, died,
 his daughter Hafsa, a widow of the Prophet, kept the
 original recorded Quran. During the reign of ‘Uthman
 15

===== PDF PAGE 32 =====
ibn ‘Affan, one of his emissaries to Syria and Iraq, named
 Huzeifa ibn Al-Yam4n, came to him and informed him
 of serious variations in reciting the Qur’an by the
 Muslims of Syria and Iraq. Huzeifa said to ‘Uthman
 ‘O prince of the faithful, save this nation before they
 distort their scripture as the Christians and Jews have
 distorted theirs.” ‘Uthman immediately sent for the
 Qur’an from Hafsa, and ordered the reproduction
 thereof. Four of the original revelation writers were
 charged with the reproduction work. They were Zeid
 ibn Thabit, ‘Abdullah ibn Al-Zubair, Sa‘id ibn Al-‘As
 and ‘Abdul-Rahman ibn Al-Harith ibn Hisham. The
 reproduced copies were dispatched to Basra (Iraq), Kifa
 (Iraq), Damascus (Syria), and Mecca. Other copies were
 kept in Medina.” These copies were further reproduced
 and they represent the originals for the Qur’an published
 today.

===== PDF PAGE 33 / PRINTED PAGE 17 =====
Chapter 2
 The Qur’anic Initials
 Of the 114 suras in the Qur’an, 28 begin with one
 set of Qur’anic initials, and one sura is prefixed with
 two sets of Qur’anic initials. Each set of Qur’anic initials
 consists of a number of letters ranging between one and
 five. The different sets of Qur’anic initials and the suras
 where they occur are listed in Table 4. As shown in that
 table, there are 14 different sets of Qur’anic initials, and
 the number of letters which participate in the make-up
 of these initials is also 14. The 14 letters involved in
 the formation of Qur’anic initials are Alif (A), Ha (H),
 Ra (R), Sin (S), Sad ($), Ta (T), ‘Ayn (‘A), Qaf (Q,
 Kaf (K), Lam (L), Mim (M), Nin (N), Ha (H), and
 Ya (Y). These letters are known in the Muslim literature,
 more specifically among the Sufi mystics, as “the illu-
 nation letters الريك‎ 4,,4\ .” The other half
 of the alphabet, the 14 letters not involved, are known
 as “the darkness letters azs\Ass\ الخزيف‎ .” To
 explain the reasoning behind this nomenclature, we
 would have to go beyond the scope of this book.
 The suras which contain Qur’anic initials are listed
 in their recorded sequence in Table 5. The same suras,
 listed in their chronological (revelation) sequence, are
 shown in Table 6. As we shall see later, both sequences
 are important in the final analysis of the Qur’anic initials.

Chapter 3
 REVIEW OF THE LITERATURE
 During the past 14 centuries, a number of theories
 were developed dealing with the possible significance of
 the Qur’anic initials. The most comprehensive reviews
 of these theories are those reported by Al-Suyity’s famous
 reference, “Al-Itqan Fi ‘Slam Al-Qur’an,” and Al-
 Baydawy in his well-known commentary on the Qur’an.
 There is an unanimous agreement among the Muslim
 scholars who studied the Qur’anic initials that these
 “mysterious” letters have something to do with the
 miraculous nature of the Qur’an. Most of those scholars
 considered the magnificent literary composition of the
 Qur’an, and the fact that it was revealed through an
 illiterate man (the Prophet Muhammad, peace be upon
 him), to be the miracle of the Qur’an. Accordingly, it
 has been generally believed that the qur'an frequent
 challenge for anyone to produce anything like it, is a
 challenge to compose a literary work as beautiful, precise,
 and linguistically perfect as the Qur’an.
 The most predominant theories explaining the Qur’-
 anic initials can be summarized as follows:
 1. The Qur’anic initials represent a challenge for anyone
 to compose from these alphabets a literary work as
 awesome as the Qur’an.
 2. The Qur’anic initials were placed at the beginning
 of certain suras to attract the listeners’ attention.
 21

===== PDF PAGE 38 / PRINTED PAGE 22 =====
_ The Qur’anic initials are secret or symbolic names
 of God.
 , The Qur’anic initials are symbolic names of the suras
 where they occur.
 _ The Qur’anic initials are secret or symbolic names
 of the Qur’an, similar to Al-Furqan and Al-Dhikr.
 . The Qur’anic initials are words in a foreign tongue
 Al-Baydawy, however, dismissed this theory on the
 basis that the Qur’an repeatedly states that it was
 revealed in a “perfect Arabic tongue.”
 . Ibn ‘Abbas theorized that the letter Alif (A) in Alif
 Lam Mim stands for Allah (God), the Lam (L) stands
 for the angel Gabriel, and the Mim (M) stands for
 Muhammad. Thus, this set of Quranic initials informs
 us that the Qur’an came from God to the Prophet
 Muhammad (peace be upon him), through the me-
 diation of Gabriel.
 . Ibn ‘Abbas also combined three different sets of
 initials, namely, Alif Lam Ra (A.L.R.), Ha Mim
 (H.M.), and Nan (N), to spell out the word Al
 Rahman, one of God’s magnificent names.
 . The most recent and comprehensive study of the
 Qur’anic initials is that presented by ‘Abdullah Yusuf
 ‘Ali in his Qur’anic translation. His study is repro-
 duced here in its entirety:
 APPENDIX 1.
 The Abbreviated Letters (Al-Mugatie'as)
 Certain sorts have certain initials prefixed to them, which are called the
 “ Abbreviated Letters", A number of conjectures have been made as to their meaning.
 Opinions are divided as to the exact meaning of euch particular letter or combination
 of letters, but it is agreed that they have a mystic meaning.
 22

===== PDF PAGE 39 / PRINTED PAGE 23 =====
Mystic meaning, not intelligible at first sight, is not inconsistent with the
 character of the Qur-an as a“ plain book’. The book of nature is also a plain book,
 bot how few car Cully understand it? Every one can get out of the Qur-an plain guidance
 for his life according to his capacity for spiritual understanding. As his capacity grows,
 so will his understanding grow. The whole Book is a Record for alltime. It must
 necessarily contain truths that only gradually unfold themselves to humanity. Even
 parables and tales of mystic’ meaning employ symbolism. The plain man may find the
 symbolism helpful, as a soldier finds his National Flag helpful. But what proportion of
 British soldiers or citizens understands the full symbolism of the British Union Jack ?
 This is not a mystery of the same class as '' mysteries'’ by which we are asked
 to believe against the dictates of reason. If we are asked to believe that one is three
 and three is one, we can give no intelligible meaning to the words. If we are asked to
 believe that certain initials have a meaning which will be understood in the fullness of
 time or of spiritual development, we are asked to draw upon Faith, but we are not asked
 to do any violence to our reason.
 I shall tey to discuss some of the probable meanings of any particular abbrevi-
 ated letter or set of abbreviated letters on the first occasion on which it appears in the
 Qur-an. But it may be desirable here to take a general view of the facts of their occur-
 rence to help us in appreciating the various views which are held about them.
 There are 29 letters in the Arabic alphabet (counting hamas and alif as two
 letters), and there are 29 Shirts which have abbreviated letters prefixed to them. One of
 these Silas (S. xlii.) has two sets of abbreviated letters, but we need not count this Sir
 twice. If we take the half of the alphabet, omitting the fraction, we get 14, and this is
 the number of letters which actually occur in the Muqatta’at.
 The 14 letters, which occur in various combinations, are :—

 breath passes through the various organs of speech, ,عه‎ the throat (guttural), or the
 various positions of the tongue to the middle or front of the palate or to the teeth, or
 the play of the lips. Everyone of these kinds of sounds is represented in these letters.

Again the question arises: Does the ثم‎ in ZI, Be, and y= stand for the
 same signification, or does it mean a different thing in each case? We may gencralise
 and say that there are three series of six, and one series of three, and the others occur
 all singly.
 We should logically look for a common factor in the 58:35 bearing the
 same initials, and this factor should be different for Firms bearing other initials. In all
 cases where the abbreviated letters occur, there is some mention of the Qur-fn or
 the Book. The //gan makes an exception in the case of three Silas, Ankabit5 Signs
 in
 nature (xxx. 20-27) and His revelation in the Qur-dn. In 5. Xviii. the very first verse
 begins the theme with the Pen as the instrument of writing, exhorts Mustafa to stand
 forth boldly to proclaim the Message, and ends (Ixviii. 52) with the declaration that it is
 a Message for all the worlds.
 These are general considerations, which J have thought it most convenient to
 Present in the form of an Appendix.
 In his explanation of the Qur’anic initials Alif Lam
 Mim Sad, ‘Abdullah Yusuf ‘Ali said,
 989. This is a combination of four Abbreviated letters. For Abbreviated Letters generally, see
 Appendix I (at the end of SOra I). The combination here includes the three letters A. L. M., which
 occurred at the beginning of SOra Il, and are discussed inn 25 to iil.
 The additional letter Sd occurs in combination here and in Star xix, and by itself at the
 beginning of 5. xxviii, and nowhere else. The factor common to 5 vii, S. xix, and 5, xxxviii, is that
 in each case the core of the SOra consists in the stories (qisas) of the Prophets. In this Sara we have
 the stories of Noah, HOd, Salih, Lot. Shu‘atb, and Moses, leading up to Muhammad, and in S. xxxviit
 the stories of David Solomon, and Job similarly lead up to Muhammad, occupying three out of the
 five sections, Sara xix consists almost entirely of such stories. Can we understand 540 to stand for
 queas, of which it ts the most characteristic letter? In this ه502‎ vii. we have also the spiritual history
 of mankind traced,—the Beginning, the Middle, and the End, which, as explained in n. 25, might be
 represented symbolically by A.L.M. If so, this SOra, dealing with the Beginning, Middle, and End
 of man’s spiritual story. and illustrating it by the stories of the Prophets, might well be represented
 symbolically by the letters Alif, Lam, Mim, Séd. But no one can be dogmatic about these symbols.
 We must accept them as symbols with a mystic meaning.
 His explanation of the initials Alif Lam Ra was as
 follows:
 As shown in Appendix | (Spare 3), the Abbreviated Letters are mystic symbols,
 about whose meaning there is no authoritative explanation. If the theory advanced in
 .م‎ 25 to ii. 1 has any validity, and the present group A.L.R. is cognate to the group
 A.L.M., we have to consider and form some idea in our minds as to the probable
 meaning of the variation. We took A.L.M. to be a symbol of those Sdras tbat deal
 with the beginning, the middle, and the end of man’s spiritual history,—the origin,
 the present position, and the things to come in the Last Days (eschatology, to use a
 theological term). We took A.L. to stand as symbols of the first two, and M. of the
 last. In the present group of Sdras we find hardly any eschatological matter, and
 therefore we can understand the absence of M., the symbol standing for such matter.
 In its place comes R., which is phonetically allied to .ل‎ L. is produced by the impact
 of the tongue to the front of the palate, and R. to the middle of the palate. In
 25

===== PDF PAGE 42 / PRINTED PAGE 26 =====
many languages the letters L. and R. are interchangeable; ¢.g., in Arabic, al-Rahman
 becomes ar-Rahmdn, and R. in imperfect enunciation becomes L., as in Chinese
 valuations. If L. is a symbol of present-day things looking to the future, we may take
 R. as a symbol of present-day things looking within, ركع‎ into the interior of the
 organization of the Ummat. And this symbolism fits in with the subject-matter of
 the sorts in question. But no one should be dogmatic in speculation about mystic
 Symbols.
 The Alif Lam Mim Ra at the beginning of sura
 Al-Ra’ad (no. 13) was explained as follows:
 1798. For A.L.M., see it, 1.n. 25. For A.L.R., see Introduction to S.x. For abbreviated Letters
 generally see Appendix I. Here there seems to be a combination of the groups A.L M. and A.L.R
 We consider here not only the beginning (A.), the middle (L.), and the end (M_), of man's spiritual
 history, but also the immediate future of the interior of our organisation, such as it appeared to our
 Ummat towards the close of the Meccan period. But in trying to understand mystic symbolism, we
 must not be dogmatic. The befitting attitude is to say: God knows best.

 And in his introduction to sura Ta Ha (T. H.), he
 offered the following explanation:
 The mystic letters 72 Ha are prefixed to this Séra. What do they mean? The
 earliest tradition is that they denote a dialectical interjection meaning “O man!" If
 so, the title is particularly appropriate in two ways. (1) It was direct and personal
 address to a man in a high state of excitement, tempted by his temper to do grievous
 wrong, but called by God's Grace, as by a personal appeal, to face the realities, for

 Gracious, out of His Grace and Mercy (xx. 5). (2) It takes up the story from the

 story of Moses in the crisis of his life when he received God's Commission and in his
 personal relations with his mother, and how he came to be brought op in the Pharaoh's
 house, to learn all the wisdom of the Egyptians, for use in God's service, and in his
 personal relations with Pharaoh, whom we take to be his adoptive father (xxviii. 9).
 It further tells the story of a fallen soul who misled the Israelites into idolatry, and
 recalls how man's Arch-enemy Satan caused his fall. Prayer and praise are necessary
 to man to core his spiritual blindness and enable him to appreciate God's revelation.

Chapter 4
 DEVELOPMENT OF THIS RESEARCH
 Several hypotheses concerning the Qur’anic initials
 were developed and studied in the course of this research.
 Thus, the correlation between the Qur’anic initials and
 the names of God mentioned in the initialed sura was
 studied. Interestingly, some correlation was found, and
 it was consistent enough to warrant mention here. In
 sure Al-Baqara, for example, which is prefixed with the
 initials Alif Lam Mim (A.L.M.), it was found that the
 glorious names or attributes of God which terminate
 certain verses of this sura begin with the letters Alif Lam
 (A.L.) and end with the letter Mim (M). These names
 and their locations in sura Al-Baqara (no. 2) are shown
 in Table 7.

 Allfh,..RaGf, RahIm----------------- 143

===== PDF PAGE 46 / PRINTED PAGE 30 =====
This relationship was observed as well in sura Ali-
 ‘Imran (no. 3) which is also initialed by A.L.M.
 Moreover, the subsequent suras which contain no
 Qur’anic initials did not exhibit the same phenomenon.
 The set-back for a possible generalization of this hy-
 thesis came when a study of the other initials did not
 confirm this observation.
 A second hypothesis was tested whereby the correlation
 between the Qur’anic initials and the names of God’s
 messengers or prophets was examined. No consistent
 correlation was found, and this hypothesis was also
 discarded. Many other fruitless attempts were made to
 find some consistent pattern related to the Qur’anic
 initials and their suras. Finally, the decision was made
 to physically count all the alphabetic letters which make
 up each one of the qur'an 114 suras. In order to find
 all the possible mathematical correlations among the
 letters in general, and the Qur’anic initials in particular.
 electronic computers had to be deployed.

===== PDF PAGE 47 / PRINTED PAGE 31 =====
Chapter 5
 METHODS OF CALCULATION
 The only calculations carried out manually were the
 initial determinations of the frequency of occurence of
 each letter in each sura. A special form was prepared
 as shown in Figure 1 to facilitate the counting process.
 As shown in Figure 1, the name of the sura, the number
 of verses in that sura, its chronological number in the
 sequence of revelation, and its number in the sequence
 of recording were listed at the top of the form. The 28
 alphabetic letters were listed on the right-hand side of
 the form on a vertical line. The counting was carried
 out by reading the sura while placing a short vertical
 line in front of each letter read. Thus, as
 was read, a vertical mark was placed in front of the
 letter Ba, then in front of the letter Sin, then in front
 of the letter Mim, then in front of the letter Alif, then
 two marks were placed in front of the letter Lam, and
 so forth. Every fifth mark was a slash across the previous
 four marks. When a sura was completed, the number
 of each letter was obtained by counting the marks in
 front of it, and the total number of alphabetic letters
 was calculated by adding up the numbers of all 28 letters.
 The final result for sura Al-Fatiha (no. 1) is illustrated
 in Figure 2. All subsequent calculations were carried out
 by means of a Xerox Data System’s Sigma 7 electronic
 computer, utilizing a teletype terminal (Time-Share
 31

===== PDF PAGE 48 / PRINTED PAGE 32 =====

 33

===== PDF PAGE 50 / PRINTED PAGE 34 =====
Network). All programing was done in FORTRAN IV
 language. A library program of Com-Share, Inc. entitled
 “MARS” was used for the arrangement of the final data
 in ascending order.
 For each of the 14 letters which participate in the
 Qur’anic initials, the following three values were de-
 determined:
 1. The Absolute Frequency: This was simply the number
 of occurrences of each letter in each sura.
 2. The Percentage of Frequency: This value was calculated
 by dividing the frequency of occurrence of each letter
 by the total number of letters in the sura, then
 multiplying the product by 100.
 3. The Average Per Verse Value: This was determined by
 dividing the absolute frequency of a given letter by
 the number of verses which constitute the sura.
 For a multi-lettered set of Qur’anic initials, these three
 values were determined by adding up the corresponding
 values of all the constituent letters. Thus, the absolute
 frequency values were added together, the percentage
 values were added together, and the average per verse
 values were added together. To illustrate, let us consider
 sura Al-Baqara. This sura is made up of 286 verses, with
 the first verse consisting of three alphabetic letters, Alif
 Lam Mim (A.L.M.). The total number of letters in this
 sura is 25,717. The absolute frequency of occurrence of
 the letter Alif (A) is 4,329. This makes the percentage
 value of the letter Alif (A) in sura Al-Baqara =
 (4,329/25,717) x 100 = 16.8%. The average per verse
 34

===== PDF PAGE 51 / PRINTED PAGE 35 =====
occurrence of the letter Alif (A) in sura Al-Baqara would
 be 4,329/286 = 15.14. The corresponding values for the
 letter Lam (L) are 2,941, 11.4%, and 10.28. For the letter
 Mim (M), the three values were determined as 2,195,
 8.5%, and 7.67, respectively. Thus, the absolute frequency
 value for the set of initials Alif Lam Mim (A.L.M.) was
 determined by adding 4,329 + 2,941 +2,195 = 9,465.
 Similarly, the percentage value of ALM in sura AI-
 Baqara was determined by adding 16.8 11.4
 +8.5 = 36.7%. The average per verse value of ALM
 in Al-Baqara was calculated as 15.14+10.28
 + 7.67 = 33.09. Of course, all these computations were
 carried out by the computer. The three values for each
 one of the Qur’anic initials in each one of the 114 suras
 were thus determined.
 The reason for following this particular method for
 calculation of the multi-lettered Qur’anic initials is the
 assertion by Prophet Muhammad (peace be upon him)
 that their constituent letters are independent of each
 other. This was in a hadith reported by Al-Bukhary,
 Al-Baydawy, and Al-Suyity. The Prophet said, “I do
 not say that Alif Lam Mim (A.L.M.) constitutes one
 entity. I say that Alif (A) is an independent letter, Lam
 (L) is an independent letter, and Mim (M) is an in-
 dependent letter.”

 which participates in Qur’anic initials, in each sura.
 These letters are Alif (A), Ha (H), Ra (R), Sin (S),

2. The total number of letters in each sura.
 3. The number of verses in each sura.
 The computer was programmed to perform the fol-
 lowing functions:
 1. Compute the percentage value for each one of the
 14 letters in each of the 114 suras.
 2. Compute the average frequency of occurrence of each
 one of the 14 letters in each verse of each sura.
 3. Compute the absolute frequency of occurrence of the
 multi-lettered Qur’anic initials, namely Ta Ha (T.H.),
 Ta Sin (T.S.), Ya Sin (Y.S.), Ha Mim (H.M.), Alif
 Lam Mim (A.L.M.), Alif Lam Ra (A.L.R.), Ta Sin
 Mim (T.S.M.), Alif Lam Mim Sad (A.L.M.S.), Alif
 Lam Mim Ra (A.L.M.R.), Kaf Ha Ya ‘Ayn Sad
 (K.H.Y.‘A.S.), and Ha Mim-‘Ayn Sin Qaf (H.M.-
 ‘A.S.Q.). Table 9 is a computer printout listing the
 absolute frequency of occurrence of the Qur’anic
 initials in each sura.
 4. Compute the percentage of frequency value for each
 of the multi-lettered initials in each of the 114 suras.
 Table 10 is a computer printout of the percentage
 frequency values of all the Qur’anic initials throughout
 the Qur’an.
 5. Compute the average occurrence per verse for each
 of the multi-lettered Qur’anic initials in each sura.
 These data are presented in Table 11 which is a
 computer printout of the average per verse values for
 each of 14 Quranic initials in each one of the qur'an
 114 suras.
 38

===== PDF PAGE 55 / PRINTED PAGE 39 =====
6. Arrange all the suras of Qur’an in ascending order
 with regard to the absolute frequency of occurrence
 of each Qur’anic initial. Thus, for each one of the
 Qur’anic initials, the sura which contains the lowest
 frequency of occurrence appears at the top of the list,
 and the sura containing the highest frequency appears
 at the bottom of the list. It was initially intended to
 have the computer list the suras in a “descending”
 order, to have the sura with the highest value at the
 top, rather than the bottom of the list. However, due
 to a programming error, this particular command to
 the computer became reversed. This of course does
 not affect the results or the conclusions in the least.
 7. Arrange the 114 suras of the Qur’an in ascending order
 with regard to the percent frequency of occurrence
 for each of the 14 sets of Qur’anic initials.
 8. Arrange all suras of the Qur’an in ascending order
 with regard to the average occurrence in each verse
 of each sura. The computer printouts of all the lists
 are shown within the text, and will be referred to as
 required.
 In searching for a single value which will represent
 each one of the Qur’anic initials most accurately, the
 percentage value stands out as the best possible choice.
 Certainly, we cannot use the absolute frequency of
 occurrence of a given letter, or a set of letters, as a
 representative measure or as a basis for comparing the
 various suras. For example, we cannot compare a long
 sura such as Al-Baqara (286 verses, 25,717 alphabetic
 39

===== PDF PAGE 56 / PRINTED PAGE 40 =====
letters), with a short sura such as Al-Nas (3 verses, 99
 alphabetic letters) on the basis of absolute frequency of
 occurrence. Similarly, the average per verse value cannot
 be used as a truly representative measure of the frequency
 of occurrence of the Qur’anic initials. The percentage
 value, therefore, appears to be the only value which can
 be used as a basis for comparison. However, we will have
 to take into consideration that the percentage value does
 not ALWAYS offer a good representation of the fre-
 quincy value. It can be distorted by such factors as the
 lack of sufficient component units (statistically known
 as observations). To illustrate this point, let us take the
 extreme example of a chapter which consists of only the
 two letters A and B. The percent of each of these letters
 would be 50%, an extraordinarily high percentage value.
 If we compare the letter A in each chapter of the book,
 the hypothetical two-lettered chapter undoubtedly will
 have the highest percentage value (50%). But we cannot
 honestly conclude from this comparison that this two-
 lettered chapter “has the highest frequency of the letter
 A.” In this study, we will come across instances where
 the percentage value is obviously distorted by the extreme
 shortness of certain suras. Whenever the percentage value
 is questionable (as a true representative of the frequency).
 we will have to consider carefully all three values of the
 Quranic initial under study (the absolute number, the
 percentage, and the average per verse value). These
 instances and the computations involved in them will
 be discussed in detail wherever the occasion arises in
 the text.
 40

===== PDF PAGE 57 / PRINTED PAGE 41 =====
Additionally, since different letters make up different
 sets of Qur’anic initials, we do not expect all the initials
 to exhibit one and the same phenomenon (e.g. the
 frequency of occurrence). Thus all three values may be
 needed under certain circumstances as we study the
 Qur’anic initials.
 41

===== PDF PAGE 74 / PRINTED PAGE 60 =====
Chapter 6
 RESULTS, OBSERVATIONS, AND DISCUSSION
 1. THE QUR’ANIC INITIAL Qaf (Q):
 This initial occurs in two suras. One sura, namely,
 Qaf (Q), bears the same initial as its title. It is sura
 number 50 in the recorded sequence of Qur’an; number
 34 in the chronological sequence of revelation (see Tables
 2 and 3). The other sura is Al-Shara (Consensus) which
 is initialed with two different sets of letters; the first verse
 is made up of the Qur’anic initials Ha Mim (H.M.),
 and the second verse consists of the letters ‘Ayn Sin Qaf
 (‘A.S.Q,). It is sura number 42 in the recording sequence,
 and ranks 62 in the chronological sequence of revelation.
 Table 12 is a computer printout of the percentage
 values of the letter Qaf (Q) in each one of the qur'an
 114 suras. The suras are arranged in ascending order
 with regard to the percent frequency of the letter Qaf.
 For the reader’s convenience, the titles of suras have been
 added onto the computer lists.
 The reader will note that sura Qaf (Q) occupies po-
 action number 111 out of the 114 listings. This means
 that sura Qaf (Q) contains the highest percentage of the
 letter Qaf (Q) than any other sura in the Qur’an, with
 the exception of three suras. The only suras which contain
 a higher percentage of Qaf (Q) are Al-Shams (The Sun).
 Al-Qiyama (Resurrection), and Al-Falaq (Daybreak).

Careful examination of these three suras reveals that they
 are extremely short. This fact raises the possibility that
 the percentage value of Qaf might be distorted out of
 true representation of frequency due to the extreme
 shortness of these suras. This type of statistical distortion
 was explained on page 40. Furthermore, the careful ex-
 amination reveals that all three suras are vastly inferior
 to sura Qaf in the overall frequency value of the letter
 Qaf (Q). Thus, both the absolute frequency of Qaf (Q),
 and the average per verse value are lower in these three
 suras than in sura Qaf. Table 13 is a computer printout
 of the absolute frequency values of Qaf, and Table 14
 is a computer printout of the average per verse values
 of Qaf throughout the Qur’an. Table 15 shows the
 detailed determination of the overall frequency of the
 letter Qaf in sura Qé&f, and in the three short suras
 exhibiting a higher percentage of Qaf. We note from
 Tables 13 and 15 that sura Al-Shams (The Sun) contains
 a total of 10 Qafs compared to 57 in sura Qf. Similarly,
 we note that sura Al-Qiyama (Resurrection) contains 27
 Qafs (Q’s), and sura Al-Falaq (Daybreak) contains but
 6 Qafs.

From Tables 14 and 15 we note that the average per
 verse value of the letter Qaf in sura Qaf is 1.267. The
 corresponding value in sura Al-Shams is 0.667; in sura
 Al-Qiyama it is 0.675, and in sura Al-Falaq it is 1.200.
 The net OVERALL frequency, derived from all three
 values of the letter Qaf in the four suras, is presented
 in Table 15. The computation was carried out by de-
 training the magnitude of difference above and/or
 below sura Qaf with regard to each one of the three
 values, in each sura. The magnitude of difference was
 computed by deducting the smaller value from the larger
 value, then dividing the difference by the smaller value.
 A plus sign would indicate that the difference is above
 the reference sura (sura Qaf in this case), and a minus
 sign would indicate that the difference is below the
 reference sura. To illustrate, let us compare sura A which
 contains 75 occurrences of a given letter with sura B
 which contains 25 occurrences of the same letter. The
 magnitude of difference in this case equals (75—25)/
 25 = 2.000. If sura A were the reference sura, a plus
 sign should precede the magnitude of difference
 ) + 2.000(. This means that the absolute frequency of the
 letter in question is higher in sura A than in sura B
 by a two-fold magnitude. If sura B were the reference
 sura, a minus sign would precede the 2.000.
 By comparing the net overall frequency of the letter
 Qaf in sura Qaf with the overall frequency in the three
 suras exhibiting a higher percentage value, we note that
 sura Qaf is actually more than 5 times greater than
 68

===== PDF PAGE 83 / PRINTED PAGE 69 =====
sura Al-Shams, almost two times greater than sura
 Al-Qiyama, and more than 7 times greater than sura
 Al-Falaq (see Table 15). These observations lead us to
 the conclusion that the only three suras in the Qur’an
 which exhibit a higher percentage of 034 than that in
 sura Qaf are in fact inferior to sura Qaf in the overall
 frequency. This means that SURA QAF CONTAINS
 THE HIGHEST FREQUENCY OF THE LETTER
 QAF THAN ANY OTHER SURA IN THE QUR’AN.
 What does this startling observation mean? For one
 thing, it means that someone knew that a particular sura
 will have the highest overall frequency value of the letter
 Qaf. To indicate this knowledge, this particular sura was
 “tagged” with the letter Qaf, and also entitled “Qaf.”
 The next logical question would be, “Who is that
 ‘SOMEONE”” Who could possibly acquire advance
 knowledge about the distribution of the alphabet
 throughout the Qur'an? As we ask these questions, we
 should bear in mind that the Qur’an was revealed over
 a period of 23 years, and that the verses within each
 sura were often widely separated both in time and place.
 It takes little thinking to realize that ONLY GOD
 ALMIGHTY can acquire such knowledge. Perhaps this
 explains to us the wisdom behind revealing the Qur’an
 in this particular fashion, coupled with deliberate con-
 cement of the significance of the Qur’anic initials. This
 point will be explained in detail in the Epilogue section
 of this book. Within sura Qaf, one verse, namely, verse
 number 38, was revealed in Medina, more than 300 miles
 69

===== PDF PAGE 84 / PRINTED PAGE 70 =====
and many years away from other verses of this Meccan
 sura. Yet, this one verse contains two Qafs, a higher than
 average occurrence (as we know, the average per verse
 value of the letter Qaf in sura Qaf is 1.267).
 The only alternative to this conclusion would identify
 that “SOMEONE” as the Prophet Muhammad (peace
 be upon him). This alternative conclusion, however, will
 have to assume that the Prophet, an illiterate man, had
 an advance knowledge of the distribution pattern of the
 letter Qf and its exact proportions throughout 4
 chapters to be revealed in scattered verses over the next
 23 years. The reader can easily see the farfetchedness
 of such a conclusion. And we are yet talking about only
 one, the first, of 14 Qur’anic initials. Even without going
 any further in this study, we already have sufficient
 evidence that ONLY GOD COULD BE THE AU-
 THOR OF QUR’AN.
 Lest someone may actually claim that the mathe-
 magical superiority of sura Qaf in the overall frequency
 value of the letter Qaf is coincidental, God Almighty
 provided further evidence supporting the observed su-
 priority. Thus, the only other sura in the Qur’an which
 bears the letter Qaf as a Qur’anic initial, namely, sura
 Al-Shara (Consensus; No. 42), contains 57 Qafs. This
 is the same frequency of occurrence found In sura loaf
 (see Table 13). Furthermore, when we add the 57 Qafs
 of sura Qéaf plus the 57 Qafs of sura Al-Shira, the product
 is 114, same as the total number of suras in the Qur’an.
 If the letter “Q” stands for “Qur'an,” we have in these
 70

===== PDF PAGE 85 / PRINTED PAGE 71 =====
two Qaf-initialed suras one Qaf for each sura. The clear
 message here is that all 114 suras constitute the Qur’an,
 the whole Qur’an, and nothing but the Qur’an.
 The importance of this observation, and all the other
 observations in this study, is the fact that anyone can
 SEE them for himself. No guesswork or speculation is
 involved. Thus, anyone anywhere in the world can pick
 up a copy of the Qur’an, and count the letter Qaf in
 sura Qaf and in sura Al-Shira, the only suras initialed
 with the letter Qaf. This process which takes no more
 than 30 minutes would unveil the physical, factual,
 observation that these two suras contain the same number
 of Qafs (Q’s), 57. In just 30 minutes, anyone in the world
 can obtain for himself this observation which is no less
 significant than the previous observation concerning
 overall mathematical superiority. In just 30 minutes,
 anyone in the world can SEE FOR HIMSELF physical
 evidence that the Qur’an is God’s unaltered word. In
 these two Qaf-initialed suras, the presence of one more
 word (or one less) such as Qul (say), or Huq (truth),
 or Qawm (folk), or any substitutions or alterations thereof
 during the past 14 centuries would have nullified this
 significant observation.

2. THE QUR’ANIC INITIAL Nan (N):
 lam (The
 This initial occurs in one sura entitled Al-Qa
 Pen). It is sura number 68 in the recorded sequence of
 suras; number 2 in the chronological sequence of reve:
 lation. Tables 16, 17, and 18 are the computer printouts
 of the percentage values of Nun (N), the absolute fre-
 quincy of occurrence values, and the average per verse
 values in all suras, respectively. The suras are arranged
 ending order with regard to these values. By looking
 in asc
 at the list of percentage values of Nan (Tabl
 e 16), we
 note that sura Al-Qalam has a very high percentage of
 Nan (N). It occupies position number 109 out of the
 114 listings. This means that sura Al-Qalam (The Pen),
 which is initialed with the letter Nin, has the highest
 percentage of Nin, with the exception of only 5 suras.
 However, careful examination of these five suras reveals
 that three of them are suras which contain Qur’anic
 initials. These three suras are Al-Shu‘ara’ (Poets), Al-
 Dukhan (Air Pollution), and Al-Hijr (The Hijr Valley).
 The Qur’anic initials which prefix these suras are Ta
 Sin Mim (T.S.M.), Ha Mim (H.M.), and Alif Lam Ra
 (A.L.R.), respectively. Moreover, we note that all three
 suras belong to “grdéups” of suras which bear identical
 Qur’anic initials. Thus, sura Al-Shu‘ara’ belongs to the
 Ta Sin Mim group which includes sura Al-Qasas
 (History). Sura Al-Dukhan belongs to the Ha Mim group
 which includes six other sister suras (see Tables 4, 5,
 and 6). Sura Al-Hijr has 4 sister suras which bear the
 initials Alif Lam Ra. As this study progresses, we will
 72

===== PDF PAGE 87 / PRINTED PAGE 73 =====
find that the suras with identical initials must be treated
 as one unit, as if they were one continuous sura. When
 we combine the three suras under study here with their
 sister suras, we note that the average percentage of the
 letter Nin in the unified groups is indeed inferior to
 the percentage contained in sura Al-Qalam. These
 averaging computations are shown in Table 19.
 We already note that the mathematical superiority of
 sura Al-Qalam with regard to its initial (Nin), is not
 as strong as that of sura Qaf with regard to the letter
 Qaf. We also note that while sura Qaf is entitled “Qaf”
 in addition to having Qaf as a Qur’anic initial, this is
 not the case with sura Al-Qalam. This Nin-initialed sura
 is not entitled “Nan.” One expects the different Qur’anic
 initials to exhibit different characteristics. Thus, we find
 the requirement for averaging the suras with identical
 initials in the case of Nin (N). As the reader recalls,
 no such averaging was required to see the superiority
 of the Qur’anic initial Qaf. It is through this diversity,
 and precision, that the miracle of the Qur’an becomes
 manifest as this study proceeds. Also, this phenomenon
 provides us with a possible answer to the question, “Why
 do certain suras bear identical initials?”
 After satisfying the requirement for averaging percent
 Nan (N) in the sister suras which contain the initials
 Ta Sin Mim, Ha Mim, and Alif Lam R4, we note that
 only two suras in the whole Qur’an contain a higher
 percentage of Nin than sura Al-Qalam. These two suras
 are Al-Saffat (The Lineup), and Al-Wagqi‘a (The Nev-
 73

===== PDF PAGE 88 / PRINTED PAGE 74 =====
table). Yet, by studying Table 18, we note that both
 suras are inferior to sura Al-Qalam in the average per
 verse occurrence of the letter Nan. While sura Al-Qalam
 contains on the average 2.558 Niins (N’s) in each verse.
 sura Al-Saffat contains an average 2.385 Nine per verse.
 and sura Al-WAqi‘a contains an average 2.014 Nine in
 ‘each verse. Thus, the mathematical superiority of the
 Nin-initialed sura (Al-Qalam) with regard to the fre-
 quincy of the letter Nin is unquestionable. After sat-
 saying the requirement for averaging the percentage
 value of Nin in the suras with identical initials, we find
 that THERE IS NOT ONE SURA IN THE WHOLE
 QUR’AN WHERE ALL THREE VALUES OF NUN
 ARE HIGHER THAN THEIR COUNTERPARTS IN
 THE NUN-INITIALED SURA, AL-QALAM.

===== PDF PAGE 96 / PRINTED PAGE 82 =====
3, THE QUR’ANIC INITIAL Sad (5):
 revealed
 This initial occurs in two suras which were
 consecutively, but separated in the recording sequence.
 The first sura is entitled “Sad ($),” and it ranks 38 both
 r. The second
 in the revelation order and recording orde
 sura is Al-A‘raf (The Purgatory) which ranks 39 in the
 revelation sequence, and 7 in the order of recording. Sura
 Sad has the letter Sad as the only initial, while sura
 Al-A‘raf bears three other letters as Qur’anic initials,
 namely, Alif Lam Mim Sad (A.L.M.S.). The computer
 printouts of the percentage values of the letter Sad, its
 absolute frequency of occurrence values, and its average
 per verse values, are shown in Tables 20, 21, and 22,
 respectively. By examining the list of percentage values,
 the reader will note that sura Sad ranks 88 out of the
 114 suras. Further examination reveals that the suras
 which exhibit a higher percentage of Sad than sura Sad
 are all, without exception, very short suras. This fact
 is illustrated in Table 23 which lists these suras and their
 content of the letter Sad. For sure, by studying the three
 computer listings (Tables 20, 21, and 22), we note that
 THERE IS NOT ONE SURA IN THE WHOLE
 QUR’AN WHERE ALL THREE VALUES OF THE
 LETTER SAD ARE HIGHER THAN THEIR
 COUNTERPARTS IN SURA SAD.
 As previously illustrated on page 40, the extreme
 shortness of these suras may contribute to a distortion
 of their percentage values beyond true representation of
 the frequency. This warrants a detailed look at the
 82

===== PDF PAGE 97 / PRINTED PAGE 83 =====
different values of Sad in each one of these short suras.
 The overall frequency value of the letter Sad and the
 net magnitudes of difference in relation to sura Sad have
 been computed as explained on page 68. The results are
 shown in Table 24. This table shows that there are three
 suras which carry a plus sign, i.e., they are superior to
 sura Sad in the net overall frequency of the letter Sad.
 Therefore, although sura Sad contains a very high
 relative frequency of the letter Sad, its superiority is not
 absolute. It has the highest overall frequency of the letter
 Sad than any other sura in the Qur’an, with the exception
 of the three suras indicated in Table 24, namely, Al-
 Hashr (The Thronging), Al-Mulk (Kingship), and Al-
 ‘Asr (The Afternoon). These data remind us that the
 letter Sad, as a Qur’anic initial, covers not only sura
 Sad, but also sura Al-A‘raf (The Purgatory) which is
 a continuation of sura Sad as far as the letter Sad is
 concerned (chronologically, these two suras rank 38 and
 39. See Tables 3 and 6). The percentage of the letter
 Sad is 0.954 in sura Sad, and 0.688 in sura Al-A‘raf.
 The average of these two values is 0.821%. Using this
 average, all suras with a higher than 0.821 percentage
 of Sad were studied in detail as shown in Table 25. All
 three values of Sad in these suras were considered, and
 the magnitudes of difference above and below the cor-
 responding average of (sura Sad + sura Al-A‘raf) were
 computed as explained previously. As it turned out, only
 one sura, Yusuf (Joseph), exhibited a plus sign for the
 net overall frequency of Sad (see Table 25). But sura
 83

===== PDF PAGE 98 / PRINTED PAGE 84 =====
Yusuf itself is an initialed sura which belongs to the Alif
 Lam Ra group (see ‘! able 4). When this sura is considered
 with its sister suras, we find that the average percentage
 of Sad is indeed lower than in sura Sad or sura Al-A‘raf
 (Table 26). Thus, by averaging the percentage value of
 Sad in the five A.L.R.-suras, we observe that the letter
 Sad has its highest relative frequency in the united suras
 Sad/Al-A‘raf which are initialed with the letter Sad.
 1 This observation teaches. us that the existence of the
 letter Sad as a Qur’anic initial in sura Al-A‘raf is no
 accident. WE LEARN THAT EVERY SINGLE LET.
 TER IN THE ENTIRE QUR’AN WAS CAREFULLY
 MEASURED AND CALCULATED. As we saw from
 these data, sura Sad by itself did not exhibit absolute
 superiority with regard to the initial Sad. We were unable
 to ignore the continuation of the Qur’anic initial Sad
 into the subsequent sura entitled Al-A‘raf. Since sura
 Al-A‘raf contains three other initials besides 530, namely,
 Alif (A), Lam (L), and Mim (M), a detailed discussion
 of this sura will follow later.
 84

===== PDF PAGE 109 / PRINTED PAGE 95 =====
4. THE QUR’ANIC INITIALS Ta Ha (T.H.):
 This set of initials occurs in one sura whose title consists
 of the same two letters. Sura Ta Ha is the twentieth
 chapter in the recording order of Qur’an, while its
 chronological order of revelation is 45. The percentage
 occurrence of Ta Ha, the absolute frequency of occur-
 rence, and the average occurrence per verse values are
 shown in Tables 27, 28, and 29, respectively. We note
 from the percentage list that sura Ta Ha clearly has
 a very high relative frequency of the letters Ta Ha. To
 find out what special characteristics sura Ta Ha might
 have with regard to its Qur’anic initials, a closer look
 at the suras with higher percentages of Ta Ha is war-
 ranted. Computation of the overall frequency value of
 Ta Ha was carried out for these suras, and the results
 are shown in Table 30. We note from this table that
 five suras are superior to sura Ta Ha in the overall
 frequency value of the letters Ta Ha. These five suras
 are Al-Hajj (Pilgrimage), Al-Nir (The Light), Al-Fat-h
 (Victory), Al-Muja-dala (The Argument), and Al-Tawba
 (Repentance). But we also note that all these five are
 Medinan suras. No Meccan sura is superior to sura Ta
 Ha in the overall frequency of the letters Ta Ha. For
 sure, by examining the three computer listings in Tables
 27, 28, and 29, we find that there is not one Meccan
 sura in the whole Qur’an where all three values of Ta
 Ha are higher than their counterparts in sura Ta Ha.
 From these data, therefore, we observe that SURA TA
 HA IS SUPERIOR TO ALL MECCAN SURAS with
 95

===== PDF PAGE 110 / PRINTED PAGE 96 =====
regard to the Qur’anic initials Ta Ha. The reader is
 reminded that sura Ta Ha is a Meccan sura. Together
 with later data in this study, this set of initials serves
 to confirm for us the location of revelation of the various
 suras. This conclusion will be discussed in detail else-
 where.
 We should also note that the letter Ha occurs as an
 initial in sura Maryam which precedes sura Ta Ha, both
 in the recording sequence and the chronological se-
 quence. However, combining the values of Ha in these
 two suras does not impart any changes on the final results
 or conclusions.
 96

5. THE QUR’ANIC INITIALS Ya Sin (Y.S.):
 This set of initials occurs in one sura whose title is
 also Ya Sin (Y.S.). Sura Ya Sin ranks 36 in the recorded
 sequence of the Qur’an; 41 in the chronological sequence
 of revelation. The percentage values of Ya Sin, the
 absolute frequency values, and the average per verse
 values, throughout the Qur’an, are shown in Tables 31,
 32, and 33, respectively. Although this book may not
 make use of certain data and/or tables presented here,
 the data are provided in hopes that many other people
 will use them eventually to discover new facts relating
 to the miraculous composition of the Qur’an. Un-
 undoubtedly, this book only scratches the surface; merely
 uncovers the tip of a huge iceberg. Scholars who are
 much more versed in mathematics than this author may
 reach better methods of computation and/or value
 representation. Therefore, all the raw data are included
 in this book.
 When we look at Table 31, we note right away that
 sura Ya Sin has an extremely low relative frequency of
 the two letters Ya Sin (Y.S.). The only suras in the whole
 Qur’an which contain a lower percentage of Ya Sin are
 five extremely short suras. These five suras are Al-‘Asr
 (The Afternoon), Al-Falaq (Daybreak), Al-Kawthar
 (Bounty), Al-Shams (The Sun), and Al-Kafiran (The
 Disbelievers) (see Table 31). The total number of al-
 diabetic letters in these five suras are 90, 90, 61, 256.
 and 114, respectively. As illustrated on page 40, this
 extreme shortness leads to distortion of the percentage
 104

===== PDF PAGE 119 / PRINTED PAGE 105 =====
value as a statistically representative value for the fre-
 quincy of occurrence.
 It appears, therefore, that the special significance of
 sura Ya Sin is that it has the lowest relative frequency
 of the letters Ya Sin. This is of course a complete reversal
 of the observations made so far about the Qur’anic
 initials. It may be relevant here to note that the initials
 Ya Sin themselves represent a reversal in the alphabetical
 sequence. The first letter in this set of Qur’anic initials,
 Ya, is the LAST letter of the Arabic alphabet (see Table
 1). The letter Sin, on the other hand, is a relatively early
 letter in the alphabetical sequence. When we survey the
 other Qur’anic initials, we find that the first two initials
 usually consist of an early letter in the common sequence
 of the Arabic alphabet, followed by a late letter. Thus,
 we observe this pattern in the Qur’anic initials Ta—> Ha,
 Ha->Mim, Alif->Lam Mim, Alif>Lam Ra, Alif
 جه‎ Lam Mim Sad, Alif>Lam Mim Ra, Kaf—->Ha Ya
 ‘Ayn Sad, and Ha-> Mim ‘Ayn Sin Qaf. Only Ya<-Sin,
 and Ta~<-Sin represent a reversal of this pattern. We
 have seen in this section that the mathematical super-
 priority phenomenon is reversed as far as the initials Ya
 Sin in sura Ya Sin are concerned. We shall see later
 that the initials Ta Sin and Ta Sin Mim also exhibit
 unique mathematical phenomena.
 It should be pointed out, however, that although the
 common arrangement for the Arabic alphabet is the one
 shown in Table 1, as well as in Webster’s New Collegiate
 Dictionary, there have been different arrangements
 105

===== PDF PAGE 120 =====
during the ancient, pre-Qur’anic times. The observation
 concerning alphabet reversals in Ya Sin, therefore, may
 be challenged by certain critics. In any case, this par-
 ticular observation, or lack of it, has no effect whatsoever
 on the mathematical properties of sura Ya Sin.

6. THE QUR’ANIC INITIALS Ha Mim (H.M.):
 This set of Qur’anic initials occurs in seven Meccan
 suras which are recorded consecutively (40 through 46),
 and were revealed consecutively (60 through 66). These
 seven suras are Ghafir (Forgiver), Fussilat (Elucidated),
 Al-Shira (Consensus), Al-Zukhruf (Vanities), Al-Dukhan
 (Air Pollution), Al-Jathiya (Amenability), and Al-Ahqaf
 (The Dunes). One of these suras, namely, Al-Shira
 (number 42), contains a second set of initials, namely,
 ‘Ayn Sin Qaf (‘A.S.Q.), and will be studied in further
 detail later.
 The computer printouts of the percentage values of
 Ha Mim throughout the Qur’an, the absolute frequency
 values, and the average per verse values are shown in
 Tables 34, 35, and 36. The three values for each of the
 seven Ha Mim suras have been grouped together and
 averaged (Table 37). When we examine the list of
 percentage values we note that the seven Ha Mim suras
 are scattered throughout the list. Obviously, not all the
 Ha Mim suras are expected to exhibit mathematical
 superiority with regard to the letters Ha Mim. What
 remains to be seen, therefore, is whether or not their
 average values exhibit any special mathematical patterns.
 The average percentage of Ha Mim in the seven suras
 is 10.095 (Table 37). When we insert this average value
 in the percentage list we find that 33 out of the 114
 suras have a higher percentage of Ha Mim. Computation
 of the magnitudes of difference and the net overall
 frequency value of Ha Mim were carried out for these
 112

===== PDF PAGE 127 / PRINTED PAGE 113 =====
33 suras. Ihe results are shown in Table 38. Examination
 of this table leads to several interesting observations.
 Thus, we note that five suras have plus signs, i.e., they
 are superior to the united Ha Mim suras in the net overall
 frequency of the letters Ha Mim. These five suras are
 Hud (Prophet Hid), Al-Fat-h (Victory), Al-Mumtahina
 (The Test), Al-Rim (The Romans), and Al-Jumua‘a
 (Friday). Two of these are Meccan suras, and three are
 Medinan. But we note that the two Meccan suras (Hid
 & Al-Rium) are initialed, and that each one of them
 belongs to a group of suras which have identical initials.
 Thus, just as we grouped and averaged the Ha Mim
 suras, it appears that we have to group and average the
 other sister suras as well. It should be pointed out that
 the only Qur’anic initials which exist in consecutive suras
 are Ha Mim, Alif Lam Mim, and Alif Lam Ra. We
 see here that each one of these groups is represented,
 and required to be grouped and averaged. Thus, the
 Alif Lam Mim group is represented by sura Al-Rim,
 and the Alif Lam Ra group is represented by sura Hid
 (see Table 38). We note here that the member suras in
 each one of these three groups are mathematically de-
 pendent on each other, if we are to observe any specific
 pattern. This fact provides us with one possible expla-
 nation for having identical initials prefixing certain suras.
 The Qur’anic initials, therefore, were not placed in their
 respective suras randomly. In this study of the initials
 Ha Min, we find it necessary to group and average the
 seven Ha Mim suras, the six Alif Lam Mim suras, and
 113

===== PDF PAGE 128 / PRINTED PAGE 114 =====
the five Alif Lam Ra suras. The average percentage of
 occurrence of Ha Mim in each of these three groups
 is shown in Table 39. Having done this, we note that
 THERE IS NOT ONE SINGLE SURA IN THE
 WHOLE QUR’AN WHERE ALL THREE VALUES
 OF HA MIM ARE HIGHER THAN THEIR COUN.
 DEPARTS IN THE HA MIM SURAS.
 We also note that if we exclude the average per verse
 values, the overall frequency values of the 33 suras
 analyzed in Table 38 are found lower than the overall
 frequency of Ha Mim in the Ha Mim suras. And, if
 we take all three values into consideration (i.e., including
 the average per verse value), we find that the ONLY
 suras which are superior to the Ha Mim suras in the
 net overall frequency of Ha Mim are Medinan suras.
 As shown in Table 38, these are the three Medinan suras
 Al-Fat-h (no. 48), Al-Mum-tahina (no. 60), and Al-
 Jumu‘a (no. 62). Thus, we note here that the unified
 Ha Mim suras are SUPERIOR TO ALL THE MEC-
 CAN SURAS in the overall frequency of the letters Ha
 Mim. The reader is reminded that the seven Ha Mim
 suras are Meccan. This mathematical feature, therefore.
 contributes to the confirmation of the location of reve-
 lation, just as we saw with the Ta Ha Qur’anic initials
 (page 96).

7. THE QUR’ANIC INITIALS Ta Sin (T.S.):
 and
 8. THE QUR’ANIC INITIALS Ta Sin Mim (T.S.M.)
 This group of initials occurs in three suras which are
 consecutive both in the recording sequence and the
 chronological revelation sequence of Qur’an. Two suras
 are initialed with the three letters Ta Sin Mim. These
 are Al-Shu‘ara’ (Poets) and Al-Qasas (History). The
 Qur’anic initials constitute an independent verse in these
 two suras. The two letters Ta Sin occur in sura Al-Nam!
 (The Ant), and they make up only part of the first verse,
 rather than an independent verse. The recording num-
 bers of suras Al-Shu‘ara’ (Poets), Al-Naml (The Ant),
 and Al-Qasas (History) are 26, 27, and 28, respectively.
 The revelation numbers of these three suras are 47, 48,
 and 49, respectively. Thus, we have here three consecutive
 suras where the two-lettered set of Qur’anic initials
 separates the three-lettered sets of initials.
 There have been many discussions and speculations
 concerning the absence of Mim from the Qur’anic initials
 of the middle sura, Al-Naml. The discussion presented
 by ‘Abdullah Yusuf ‘Ali, and shown on page 27 of this
 book, is typical, and provides the reader with a good
 example of the literature treatment of this subject. Most
 books of tafsir present similar theories regarding this Ta
 Sin Mim/TA4 Sin/Ta Sin Mim arrangement.
 120

===== PDF PAGE 135 / PRINTED PAGE 121 =====
The computer printouts of the percentage values of
 Ta Sin and Ta Sin Mim, their absolute frequency of
 occurrence values, and their average per verse values
 throughout the Qur’an are shown in Tables 40 through
 45. When we look at the ascending order of percentage
 values of Ta Sin Mim (Table 40), we note that sura
 Al-Qasas (History) ranks 36, and sura Al-Shu‘ara’ (Poets)
 ranks 79, out of the qur'an 114 suras. It takes little
 thinking to realize that exactly 35 suras have a higher
 percentage of Ta Sin Mim than that of sura Al-Shu‘ara’,
 and exactly 35 suras have a lower percentage of Ta Sin
 Mim than sura Al-Qasas. Figure 3 clarifies this obser-
 vation. The arrangement of the three Ta Sin/Ta Sin
 Mim suras, both in revelation order and recording order,
 gives a special sense of balance, as can be seen in Figure
 3. The observation that 35 suras contain a higher pro-
 portion of Ta Sin Mim than sura Al-Shu‘ara’, and the
 same number of suras contain a lower percentage than
 sura Al-Qasas, leaves little doubt that the omission of
 Mim in sura Al-Naml’s Qur’anic initials was not coin-
 dental. Let us also remember that the two Ta Sin Mim
 sets of initials form an independent verse each, while
 the Ta Sin set which separates them does not make up
 an independent verse.
 The fact that this mathematical balance, manifested
 through the use of electronic computers, depends on the
 relative frequency of occurrence of Ta Sin Mim
 throughout the Qur’an is an undeniable evidence that
 the Qur’an could never be man-made, let alone man-

made by an illiterate man 14 centuries ago when the
 concepts of “percentage” and “proportion” were not
 known. In order to appreciate this miraculous phenom-
 enon, let us go back in time 1400 years. A sizeable book
 is being constructed over a period of 23 years. The
 component sentences are widely separated from each
 other both in time and place. In advance, Someone knew
 the exact relative frequency of the letters Ta (T), Sin (S),
 and Mim (M) (we have expressed this relative frequency
 by the ‘percent’ concept which was unknown in those
 days). Thus, that “Someone” knew that out of 114
 chapters, exactly 35 will have a higher relative frequency
 of Ta Sin Mim than chapter X, and exactly 35 will
 have a lower relative frequency than chapter Z. To
 indicate this fact, chapters X and Z were “tagged” with
 the letters Ta Sin Mim. And to convey the sense of
 balance inherent in this observation, chapter Y was
 “tagged” with the less powerful initials Ta Sin (only 2
 initials, and only part of the verse. See Figure 3). Ob-
 viously, this kind of advance knowledge, even if the book
 was being written within one week rather than 23 years
 and this kind of control over the distribution of individual
 alphabets, are unattainable by man.
 In spite of the remoteness of the possibility of coin-
 cadence, some critics may actually claim that this 35-
 above-and-35-below arrangement is coincidental. Just in
 case this kind of criticism may arise, God Almighty has
 provided us with one additional observation which rules
 out the possibility of coincidence. Thus, when the reader
 123

===== PDF PAGE 138 / PRINTED PAGE 124 =====
looks up the list of absolute frequency of occurrence of
 Ta Sin Mim (Table 41), he will note that sura Al-Shu‘ara’
 (XXVI) contains 615 Ta Sin Mim letters, while sura
 Al-Qasas (XXVIII) contains 580. The difference between
 the two suras is 615 - 580 = 35. This striking observation
 needs no further comment.
 The calculations and arrangement of information from
 the suras were all carried out by the computer, away
 from human bias. It would take the reader less than
 five minutes to count the number of suras above and/or
 below suras Al-Shu‘ara’ and/or Al-Qasas. It would take
 less than half an hour to count the letters Ta, Sin, and
 Mim in these two suras, and discover for one’s self that
 the difference between them is exactly 35. Thus, the
 evidence that THE QUR’AN IS THE WORD OF GOD:
 the evidence that THE QUR’AN CAN NEVER BE
 MAN-MADE, is physical, touchable, and examinable
 evidence.
 The mathematical phenomena we just observed, are
 but one facet of the multi-dimensional miracle of the
 Qur’anic initials Ta Sin/Ta Sin Mim. Thus, when we
 look at the list of percentage values of Ta Sin, we note
 that sura Al-Naml (The Ant) which is initialed by Ta
 Sin contains 2.533% of the letters Ta Sin. To find out
 whether the suras with a higher percentage are genuinely
 superior in the overall frequency of T4 Sin, the net overall
 frequency was computed as described on page 68. The
 results are shown in Table 46, and they reveal that only
 3 suras have a higher overall frequency of Ta Sin than
 124

===== PDF PAGE 139 / PRINTED PAGE 125 =====
sura Al-Naml. The first among these three suras is sura
 Yusuf (Joseph). But we note that this is an initialed sura
 which belongs to the Alif Lam Ra (ALR) group. When
 this sura is averaged with its sister suras as explained
 previously (page 72), the average percentage value of Ta
 Sin falls below that of sura Al-Naml. The second sura
 among the three suras with higher percentage is Al-Isra’
 (The Night Journey). This Meccan sura exhibits a plus
 sign in front of the net overall frequency value of TA
 Sin (Table 46). This means that sura Al-Isra’ is un-
 questionably superior to sura Al-Naml with regard to
 the Qur’anic initials Ta Sin. The third sura with a higher
 percentage is sura Ta Ha. But this sura bears the initial
 Ta in common with sura Al-Naml. Therefore, the su-
 priority of Ta Ha is nullified, since it is expected to,
 and does, have a high overall frequency in the letter
 Ta. This leaves sura Al-Isra’ as the only sura with a
 higher overall frequency than sura Al-Naml, with regard
 to the letters Ta Sin. The final conclusion from this
 analysis is that SURA AL-NAML IS SUPERIOR TO
 ALL SURAS OF THE QUR’AN IN THE OVERALL
 FREQUENCY OF THE LETTERS TA SIN, WITH
 THE EXCEPTION OF SURA AL-ISRA’.
 This observation is quite significant, when we study
 the revelation position of sura Al-Isra’. In the revelation
 sequence of Qur’an, we find that sura Al-Isra’ falls
 between the Ta Sin group of suras, and the Alif Lam

 is also superior in the letters Alif Lam Ra, even over

the Alif Lam Ra suras themselves. Only three of the
